ParlaMint-EE_2021-04-07_U89	Thank you, Mr. Speaker. Ladies and gentlemen, We have before us a seemingly simple and safe draft decision of the Riigikogu, which also refers nicely to one of the sections of the Constitution. This section states in this way § 65 (10) that the Riigikogu, acting on a proposal from the Government of the Republic, decides to grant state loans and other property obligations to the state. We were trying to give the impression here that the European Union's recovery fund is something that brings us money. Actually, it's exactly the opposite. This is a significantly larger, multi-fold commitment compared to, for example, the European stabilisation mechanism, the ESM. We are participating in a collective debt of EUR 750 billion, which will be shared by 27 European countries. This cannot be supported for four reasons. Firstly, it is a clear and screaming contradiction to the European Union's Treaties. Article 310 of the Treaty on European Union states that the European Union's expenditure and revenue must be in balance. The EUR 750 billion loan does not fit into this claim. Article 311 states that the budget is financed entirely from own resources. The loan is not an own resource. Article 125 states that a Member State is not responsible and does not assume the responsibilities of the governments of other Member States. As we have learned very clearly in this debate, that is exactly what all Member States are now going to do for each other. Secondly, the Estonian Constitution does not allow budgetary and tax policies to be handed over. However, there is no secret in the European Union that the recovery fund is a groundbreaking step towards the creation of the EU's own budget and taxes at the expense of the fiscal and budgetary sovereignty of the Member States. This is inconsistency contrary to the fundamental principles of democracy. If the Estonian electorate can no longer direct tax and budgetary policy in the elections, because a large part of our tax policy and budget resources is actually assigned to the European Union commission set up in Germany or France as a result of the elections, then there is little point in the elections in Estonia. This means that our elections will become meaningless, the opportunity for our people to direct their lives and their own money will become either very small or almost nonexistent. Thirdly, there is no fundamental change in the structure of the European Union through the creation of a recovery fund, i.e. a major leap towards the United States of Europe, in line with the mandate of the highest authority, the people, in the referendum in 2003. This mandate allowed us to belong to the Union of the Member States, and at that time we were assured that taxes, budgets, immigration, social or health policy would, in any case, remain our own. But there isn't, and now there is less. Fourthly, in the case of the recovery fund, it can be seen that agreements concluded less than a year ago are no longer being respected. In the summer of 2020, Member States agreed to a long tooth recovery fund, provided that no new taxes would be introduced by the EU for the refunder. That promise is already being violated today. It was agreed that the Fund would not be linked to national policies, i.e. not used as a political malana. This has already been violated in the case of Poland and Hungary. It was agreed that the solution would be one-off and temporary and would not change the functioning of the European Union. This agreement, too, has been violated because it is already spoken of by the Minister in Brussels, although it was clearly denied by the need to amend the Treaties with a view to making this whole thing permanent, also legal. The EU talks endlessly about the rule of law and the rule of law club. In fact, agreements are not respected and their own treaties are twisted or dullly ignored if this is considered necessary for the sake of the European project, namely centralising power. This whole thing, this whole recovery fund, it's all about fraud, lies and breach of contracts, the march that we are being offered towards the undemocratic United States of Europe. In order to preserve Estonia's sovereignty and democracy, we must categorically say no to this plan. Our group proposes to reject the draft.

ParlaMint-EE_2021-04-07_U144	Thank you. Yeah, there's been a lesson from this weekend. The lesson is that when we do large-scale vaccinations, older people, who we want to vaccinate now, first and foremost, under the conditions of vaccine deficiency, want a more personalised approach. They want them to be able to communicate with a family doctor, and they also want to be vaccinated near their home. When we look at different cities, many cities went very well over the weekend. For example, in Pärnu, the risk groups we wanted to vaccinate and all doses were delivered. There was a place, and all the elderly knew they could go there, and that's where they got the vaccine. Tallinn is so big and we found out that if vaccinations were carried out somewhere in Lasnamägi, the elderly of Nõmme would rather wait until the vaccination came near his home. An elderly person also wants to consult, he wants to go to his family doctor, who knows his previous concerns, and he wants to communicate more. So that's why we've moved all the vaccine doses that were left over from the weekend to the family doctors so they can vaccinate the groups at risk.

ParlaMint-EE_2021-04-07_U191	Thank you for that question. Indeed, Estonia has an ageing population. When I look at the numbers, in 2019 the proportion of the population aged 65 and over was 19.8%. We decided that the Minister for Social Protection, in cooperation with the Minister for Public Administration, would come up with proposals. First, to continue developing the partnership model between local authorities and the state in organising long-term care services and to present a draft law on this in March 2022. In addition, submit alternative sources of financing models for long-term care services to the meeting of the Cabinet of Government, as well as submit to the Government of the Republic a draft amendment to the law on tripartite financing of general care services provided outside the home. The whole point is to support the participation of carers in the labour market of their family members in order to reduce the burden of care and also to link the amount of financial support paid by local authorities to the actual care burden of the carer. What does that mean? This means taking into account the extent to which the work of the carer and the normal living of the normal life, as well as the resulting economic coping, has been prevented.

In fact, we treat carers as frontline workers. We do not have a national database for all the carers, but we do know those who are officially receiving care support through the local government. Indeed, this order of magnitude amounts to tens of thousands, and there may be nearly 70,000 of them. We have agreed with the family doctors that if there is a person on their list who cares for his or her loved one who wishes to receive a vaccination, he or she could be given this priority, precisely because he or she is protected by a carer who may be in a weak state of health, while also ensuring that the carer is sufficiently strong and healthy. This allows us, on the one hand, to reduce the risk of transmission of infection and, on the other hand, to ensure that the person is healthy because he, in turn, depends on the life and health of other people.

ParlaMint-EE_2021-04-07_U252	Now let's go to today's agenda. The first item is the second reading of the Draft Act 357 on the Additional Budget of 2021, initiated by the Government of the Republic. The Chair is invited by Erki Savisaar, Chairman of the Monetary Commission. Please!
ParlaMint-EE_2021-04-07_U253	Thank you, Mr President. Dear Riigikogu! The Finance Committee discussed the draft Supplementary Budget Act 2021 in preparation for the second reading, including 25, 26 and 31 March and 5 April. A number of ministers also attended the meeting of the Commission on 26 March: Minister for the Environment Tõnis Mölder, Minister for Enterprise and Technology Andres Sutt, Minister for Rural Affairs Urmas Kruuse, Minister for Culture Anneli Ott, Minister for Education and Science Liina Kersna and Minister for Public Administration Jaak Aab. Ministers gave a good overview of the additional budget in their area and also provided in writing the draft regulations for support measures or the distribution keys and the additional information requested from them on the measures. The other four ministers, who have also been given new resources with an additional budget, gave their overview already before the first reading in the Riigikogu plenary. To remind you so much, the additional budget is 641 million euros, which is intended to help Estonian residents, businesses, society and the health care system. The package of measures includes the means to pay wage subsidies in sectors where work volume decreases due to restrictions, the continuation of sickness allowance from the second day of illness, additional measures to maintain mental health, special care and rehabilitation, additional funds for medicines, vaccination and crisis management, and support measures for the tourism sector, cultural organisers and artists. By the deadline for submitting amendments, 31 March at 5.15 p.m., 11 amendments were received, including one from the Group of the Estonian Social Democratic Party, five from the Group of the Isamaa, four from the Group of the Conservative People's Party of Estonia and one from Imre Sooääre, a member of the Riigikogu who withdrew his proposal during the procedure. Explanations of the amendments can be found in the list of amendments. The proposed amendments were not supported by the government and the Commission rejected the amendments received. The main reasons why this happened were as follows. The purpose of drawing up the supplementary budget is to focus strictly on the support measures resulting from the necessary constraints and directly on the costs of the pandemic. A total of EUR 109,3 million has been planned in the state budget for 2021 to finance core R &amp; D instruments, including basic funding, research grants and public R &amp; D institutions, including additional funding of EUR 43 million by 2021. Of the additional funds, €22.4 million has been planned for the Ministry of Education and Research. Within these funds, it has been possible to prioritise activities in the budget and to cover the necessary expenditure. The additional budget for the sport sector provides for support measures of EUR 4.2 million. The support measures also reflect, inter alia, the proposed EUR 0,6 million measure for sport federations to cover their own revenues and to alleviate restrictions. However, the paragraphs in the text of the supplementary budget are not the right place to provide guidance on budget expenditure savings. Pursuant to § 28 (2) of the State Budget Act, the annual State Budget Act sets out provisions the obligation to submit the provisions of which arises from the legislation or which relate to the organisation or conditions of the collection, receipt, brokering or use of state resources in the financial year. At the end of the debate, the Commission reached a consensus on the following procedural decisions: a second reading could be concluded in this large Chamber today and the deadline for amendments would be set at 12 noon on 9 April, and when the second reading is closed, the Commission's proposal is to put the third reading of the draft and the final vote on the plenary agenda by 14 April. Thank you.

It includes, for example, wage subsidies and subsidies for hospitals and other health care institutions, as well as the costs of vaccination and testing, including the rehabilitation of scheduled patients. In the current crisis, the main challenge is for our hospitals. Our hospital staff is under an unprecedented strain, and it is therefore important that hospitals with a shortage of staff can continue to pay up to 1.5 times the remuneration of staff working in the COVID departments, with additional funding. After all, medical students have had to find time to study alongside the working shifts of exhausting corona departments. The current experience will certainly provide insight into how to further harmonise cooperation between higher education institutions and medical institutions in the future. It is also pleased that the infection rates are starting to move into a declining trend and that the vaccination rate has increased. This provides a basis for a moderate optimism in view of the future. The take-off position is quite favourable compared to the other countries of the European Union. Last year the economic downturn in Estonia was smaller than feared: 2.9%. The decrease was twice as modest as the average in the European Union. In this regard, it is fair to recall the courageous but also flexible decisions taken by the Government of Jüri Ratas. This week's spring economic forecast published by the Ministry of Finance states that domestic restrictions on economic activity and movement of people in Estonia were the least in comparison with the European Union as an average of 2020. In the early months of the crisis, companies benefited greatly from the spring pay compensation. Important support was given last year and local authorities will also receive this year. This time, municipalities will be supported by EUR 46 million. 30 million of this is investment aid and 16 million is a grant fund, broken down by basic amount and population. In Ida-Virumaa, where the negative impact on the local government's income has been higher, the grant per capita is estimated at 1.5 times. It is important to note that EUR 1 million is intended to improve the availability of internet access and digital learning materials in students' homes during the distance learning period in order to ensure equal access to education for students. It is also noteworthy that the support package for culture and sport has a capacity of 42 million, which helps to cover unavoidable costs for which no income could be earned due to restrictions. The additional budget provides for EUR 4 million to compensate for the loss of ticket revenue for commercial bus routes and the Ministry of the Interior will be able to step up the fight against the corona crisis and its spill-over effects through an additional EUR 1 million. The Centre Party Group supports the adoption of an additional budget and I would also recommend that all other ambassadors do so. Thank you very much.

ParlaMint-EE_2021-04-07_U419	Let's move on to our agenda. The next item is the second reading of the draft Act 284 on the amendment of the Maritime Safety Act initiated by the Government of the Republic and the related amendment of other laws. I call Annely Akkermann, member of the Economic Commission, as rapporteur.
ParlaMint-EE_2021-04-07_U420	Good afternoon, ladies and gentlemen. I apologise in advance that the introduction of my speech is not going to be very short, because the materials are voluminous. The draft law amending the Maritime Safety Act and related amendments to other laws was initiated by the Government of the Republic on 9 November last year. The Riigikogu took the draft 284 into procedure on 10 November. On 16 November, Kalev Kallo was appointed rapporteur by decision of the Committee on Economic Affairs. On 17 November Mikhail Korb was appointed rapporteur, and on 16 February including 16 February the draft rapporteur was appointed rapporteur. The draft amends four laws. These are the Maritime Safety Act, the Port Act, the Commercial Seafaring Act and the State Tax Act. The bill adds new definitions to the above-listed laws, waives eight licence requirements and replaces the licence in two cases by an economic activity notification. No new authorisation requirements will be added. The security requirements for ships sailing on domestic voyages and for ports serving domestic ferry routes shall be mitigated. This concerns a total of about 30 ships. With effect from 1 January 2022, the quality system of the seafarers' medical examiner shall comply with the requirements of the International STCW Convention. This Convention deals with the grounds for training, certification and watchkeeping of seafarers. From now on, the health care provider will resolve disputes about the results of the medical examination of seafarers. So far, the appeal could be filed with the Health Board. The draft establishes new and expands existing registers. These are the seafarers' register, the hydrographic information system and the navigator database. The draft provides for recognition of certificates issued by a Member State of the European Union or a Contracting Party to the Agreement on the European Economic Area, as well as by a radio operator. The Transport Authority is designated as the competent authority and can begin to issue certificates to shipowners in accordance with international conventions: the Hong Kong Convention on the Safe Recycling of Ships, the Nairobi Convention on the Removal of Ship wrecks, which was just under discussion in this hall, and the Ballast Water Convention on Ballast Water and Sludge Management. The draft specifies which ships are exempted from mandatory pilotage and takes into account the criteria for issuing certificates of competency for sea lots and senior maritime lots. Upon the entry into force of the draft, the port waters will be appointed by the Ministry of Economic Affairs and Communications instead of the Government of the Republic. The requirement of certification by the Port Security Chief Transport Board is waived and the regular security inspection of port facilities is carried out every five years instead of the current year. The draft will specify the amounts of the state fees and determine the state fees for the processing of additional requests for documents. The first reading took place in this Chamber on 25 November last year. No proposals were received from the Riigikogu on the draft by the deadline for the submission of amendments, 9 December. On 26 November, the Commission informed the Estonian Shipowners' Association in the Riigikogu, the Independent Trade Union of Estonian Seafarers, the Estonian Maritime Academy of TTÜ, the Estonian Ship Managers' Association, the Association of Estonian Small Harbours, the Logistics and Transit Association, the Estonian Loots of AS, the Estonian Ports Association, the Estonian Historical Ship Association, the Estonian Remote Fishing Association and the Estonian Chamber of Commerce and Industry. Our opinions and proposals were sent to the Commission by the Logistics and Transit Association, the Estonian Ports Association, the Estonian Shipowners' Association and the Estonian Chamber of Commerce and Industry. I will be the first to introduce the Logistics and Transit Association to the Economic Commission. In fact, the draft abolishes the obligation to authorise ship-agenting companies. In the opinion of the Logistics and Transit Association, this is not right, rather the contrary, the authorisation should be tightened up. The work of the ship's agent is to provide information related to the visit to the ship in an electronic marine information system operated by the Transport Board, but the fees paid by shipowners to ports for piloting and towing, and so on are the ship's agenting staff. The amount paid to the ship's agent for the port visits of large ships may amount to hundreds of thousands of euros, which the ship-owner pays in advance. It is difficult for foreign shipowners, in particular, to check the background of companies engaged in agency activities. This should therefore be done by the State through an authorisation obligation. In the view of the Logistics and Transit Association, a guarantee of EUR 100 000 should be imposed on the ship's agent. In its address, the Estonian Ports Association proposed to amend the Maritime Safety Act or the Commercial Seafaring Act in such a way that in Estonian territorial waters outside ports, the shipowner or charterer should not provide cargo services from his ship to another ship owner or charterer. These are STS, or ship-to-ship operations, which have been discussed recently in the press. It was also sought to add that the loading service is operated by the port managing body within or outside the port waters. It is therefore essentially intended to prohibit ship-to-ship cargoing at sea on its own initiative and not as a port service. Under the proposal, the port authority could also organise cargo operations outside the port and the port waters. The Logistics and Transit Association addressed the Economic Commission with another letter. Firstly, amendments to the Maritime Safety Act are requested so that the driving test can also be carried out in English. The master of a ship flying the flag of Estonia may also be a citizen of the European Union or of the European Economic Area. Therefore, on the basis of the principle of equal treatment, they should be allowed to take the driving test in English. This has long been the case in many countries. Secondly, it was proposed to exempt all recreational craft and vessels of less than 500 gross tonnage or less than 90 metres from pilotage. The Estonian Chamber of Commerce and Industry sent a letter of support to the Economic Commission, which supports the proposal of the Estonian Shipowners' Association and the Logistics and Transit Association to allow a driving test to be passed in English. The letter also stated that it is possible to take a driving test in several languages in Finland and Sweden, and in Riga harbour you can get a driving licence without speaking Latvian. The Estonian Shipowners' Association also once again approached the Economic Commission with a request to allow a driving test in English. It is considered that the restriction of the Estonian language is contrary to the policy of improving the competitiveness of Estonian shipping, which requires international shipping and openness. The Transport Board has been calling international ships under the Estonian flag for more than half a year, which is undoubtedly necessary for translating documents into English and advising and serving shipowners in English. In this context, the requirement that a driving test be carried out in Estonian is definitely not valid. The Committee on Economic Affairs discussed the issue of ship authorisation at a meeting held on 22 March, in which Marek Rauk, Director of Maritime and Maritime Service of the Transport Board, and Raul Tell, Head of the Department of Certification of Seafarers, participated, apart from the Deputy Secretary-General Ahti King of Transport at the Ministry of Economic and Communications. Agne Ojassaar, Legal Adviser to the Health Board, Head of Health Network of the Health System Development Department of the Ministry of Social Affairs, Heli Paluste, and Head Specialist of the same department, Mare Toompuu, were also present. The representatives of the Estonian Shipowners' Association were Indra Kaunis and Peeter Ojasaar, the Estonian pilot was represented by Peeter Sepp, and the Logistics and Transit Association was represented by Ants Ratas and Andres Valger. The aim of the meeting was to listen to the most acute questions raised during the engagement process regarding the proposals to enable you to hope to pass the driving test in English, in addition to the Estonian language, as well as the proposal to tighten the requirements for companies involved in ship-agenting. In addition, the issue was discussed in relation to the procedure for contesting the so-called seafarers' health inspection decision, which was also commented on by specialists from the Ministry of Economic Affairs and Communications who participated in the hearing. The views of market participants were heard. The decisions were taken at the next meeting on 23 March. It was agreed by consensus that the proposals received from market participants in the draft procedure would not be formulated as amendments to this draft, as an analysis of their effects and the involvement of all parties would require additional time and resources and would extend much of the procedure for the draft. Namely, Estonian Loots serves, offers pilotage service and their estimate is that up to two thirds of the pilotage service is provided on ships that regularly visit Estonian ports and if the captains and navigators were able to take the pilotage test, they would not need the pilot and this would have a significant impact on the activities of Estonian Lootsi. And the second reason was that these guarantees for EUR 100000 of ship-agenting companies are unnegotiated with market participants and may be too high for smaller market participants. It was therefore decided to take time and ask the Ministry of Economic Affairs and Communications to prepare an impact analysis by 1 September of this year, if a driving test could be expected to be taken in English. And just as by 1 September, an analysis of the need for mandatory liability insurance for ship agents was requested and assessed in the context of the rest of the world. The reason it's being discussed is still the possibility of cheating. When the Transport Board looks at ship-agenting obligations from the point of view that the ship agent provides information about the movements of ships into their marine information system, the ship agent takes as a prepayment from ships the financial obligations that it carries out in front of Estonian companies, which are ports and state. These are water charges and different taxes. The medical inspection of seafarers led to a number of discussions. As regards the procedure for challenging the medical examination of seafarers, the representatives of the initiator confirmed that the existing wording is the compromise proposed jointly by the two ministries. The Commission's definition of the person entitled to challenge remained unclear. Following the discussion, the Ministry of Economic Affairs and Communications was asked by 5 April 2021 to revise the wording of § 262 and, if necessary, to submit a further proposal for clarification. On 17 March, a letter from Minister for Economic Affairs Taavi Asa to amend and supplement draft 284 arrived at the Committee on Economic Affairs. It was sent to amend the amendments tabled and there were 8 points and 85 points on 37 pages, a large part of which was formulated during the preparation of the draft second reading following a discussion between the authors of the draft and the representatives of the lead committee. Some of the changes initially made to the draft were cancelled and added or expanded. The proposals presented by the Ministry of Economic Affairs have been combined into 29 Commission amendments by Commission official Piia Schults, and these amendments are now presented before you. All opinions and proposals received can be consulted on the draft map. At its meeting on 23 March, the Committee on Economic Affairs continued to send the draft discussion to second reading. During the consultation, a consensus was reached that the proposals received from market participants in the context of this draft procedure would not be taken into account. The Ministry of Economic Affairs had also sent its opinion not to take them into account. The large volume of proposals submitted to the Commission meeting was further discussed. At this first Commission meeting, which discussed sending the draft to first reading, Kristen Michal asked the introductions of the draft whether the draft was also in agreement with market participants. Katrin Andre replied that the draft had passed through the coordination system and its content had also been negotiated with business organisations. However, since these changes are so substantial, the Commission took procedural decisions at the hearing. This led to a longer discussion on whether it would be appropriate to conclude the second reading of the draft at the level of such amendments, given the fact that in the meantime there is a week without a meeting if market participants could read the amendments voted on by the Commission. However, in the course of the debate it was agreed that the most correct solution would be to interrupt the second reading of the draft, so that all interested parties could then have access to the formal text of the draft. Since, according to the initiator's representatives, this maritime package is a time-critical draft, the Commission considered it justified to ask the Bureau to set a shorter deadline for the submission of amendments. And since the wording of some of the amendments needed to be supplemented and considered, the committee decided to continue the discussion after the Riigikogu week. At its meeting on 5 April, the Committee on Economic Affairs continued to discuss amendments to the draft with representatives of the Ministry of Economic Affairs and Communications. The wording of the points in the two amendments was reviewed, mainly concerning the dates of entry into force. The procedural decisions of the commission shall be as follows: The Committee on Economic Affairs proposed to put the draft on the plenary agenda of the Riigikogu for a second reading by today, 7 April and to interrupt the second reading of the draft. And since the proposed deadline for the entry into force of the draft law is 1 June, it was proposed to set the deadline for amendments at 5 working days, i.e. 14 April, at 5.15 p.m. All the decisions were consensual. This is a brief summary of this long story. Thank you so much for listening.

I'll try to explain. In order to gain access to a driving test, the captain must go to a particular port at least ten times with the pilot and then have the right to go to a pilot driving test. If he does, he'll be able to drive to a particular port without any hope. And a large number of ships going to ports regularly go to them. Some take 20 trips a year, some take 40 trips. But the same captains can travel to these ports for years. Perhaps a captain of Latvia or Finland or Sweden has already travelled to that port for two hundred times, and he is well aware of that. Being on board a pilot doesn't increase the safety of navigation for him anymore. But what does hoping mean? That means there's a scooter in the harbor that takes aboard the pilot. The pilot boat has a captain, and the pilot boat has a mechanic. Three people are heading for the raid where the hope must begin, the return will bring the ship to the harbor. Once again, the same, or three people, are doing twice as much work, which, in fact, should not be done from a safety point of view, if these captains could hope to take the driving test in English, because they do not speak Estonian. But the captains of these ships, who rarely go to ports, or are new people, must visit the port ten times with the pilot anyway. The obligation to rely on them will not be reduced. However, since the number of vessels regularly sailing to ports is high, it would be the case that if these captains were allowed to take a driving test in English, the order for the Estonian pilot would be drastically reduced. This would change the possibility of the economic provision of the Estonian pilotage service. And this is where we have to calculate how these fees will change and how formations will change, and what will be the cost of hoping for those ships who do not have the right to drive.

ParlaMint-EE_2021-04-07_U449	Let's move on to the agenda. Today's third item is the first reading of the draft law 327 on the amendment of the Media Services Act initiated by the Government of the Republic and the related amendment of other laws. The rapporteur is Anneli Ott, Minister of Culture.
ParlaMint-EE_2021-04-07_U450	Mr President, I would like to thank you very much. Ladies and gentlemen in this Chamber and behind the screen! I bring to you the draft law amending the Media Services Act and related amendments to other laws. The main objective of this draft is to transpose the revised European Union Audiovisual Media Services Directive number 2018/1808, which entered into force in 2018. This directive has been transposed into Estonian national law in the past by the Media Services Act, which is why the amendments mainly concern this law. The general objective of the Directive is to ensure fair competition and equal treatment for all market participants in the audiovisual content services market. It is also one of the main objectives of updating and updating the regulation in the whole area, but also the creation of a better-functioning mechanism to protect Estonia's interests in cross-border media services, where we will be able to protect Estonia's information space and society more effectively in the event of information attacks. Both the audiovisual media market and consumption, as well as technology, have changed significantly over the last decade. The main development has been the integration of television and on-demand audiovisual media and internet services. Consumers are increasingly using services from all kinds of equipment, and broadcasters are expanding their activities online. In addition, new types of content are becoming increasingly important from audiovisual media services, which are no longer created by professional service providers, but also by users through various video-sharing platforms such as YouTube, as well as various social media channels. Media literacy is also becoming increasingly important, precisely because of the changed media landscape. Thanks to social media, all kinds of messages spread faster than ever before. Alongside media content, over which professional editorial responsibility is guaranteed, there is a large number of content created by users themselves. In such an environment, it is vital that people are able to critically assess the flow of information to them. The Ministry of Education and Research is responsible for media literacy. As mentioned, most of the changes concern the Media Services Act, but to a lesser extent the Estonian Public Broadcasting Act, the Electronic Communications Act, the Advertising Act and the State Fees Act are also amended. In summary, the main changes to the Estonian legal system. In the future, video platform managers will be included in the scope of the Act. For example, if television and on-demand audiovisual media service providers compete for the same audience's attention, their adapted regulation has so far been uneven and, for example, for video-sharing platforms, regulation has been completely lacking. One of the best known video-sharing platforms is YouTube, as well as various social media channels, most of which do not belong to the Estonian legal space. The law, including the licensing system, will be adapted to new audiovisual media services. New services include, for example, web television on major news portals. According to the current rules, online television is not even a media service because it does not meet one or the other of the conditions laid down by law. At the same time, it is actually a "classic television service'. The rules on the protection of minors and the protection of morality and legality applicable to television services and audiovisual media services on-demand shall be harmonised. In the past, they were less favourable to the on-demand service providers and gave them a competitive advantage compared to the television service provider. The most famous on-demand audiovisual media services are Netflix, Amazon Prime Video, iTunes and HBO, as well as domestic video rentals. The restrictions on the volume of television and advertising will be relaxed and made more flexible. Indeed, several strict restrictions on television advertising are no longer up-to-date as they do not allow the television service provider to earn the expected level of revenue due to changes in the advertising market. This, in turn, hampers support for the production of new audiovisual works. The advertising market is fragmented between television channels, online media and social media, so flexibility is justified. The audiovisual media service provider shall be obliged to disclose its ownership structure. This is to ensure that media consumers have the necessary background and skills to critically assess specific media content. The Ministry of Education and Research is appointed to be responsible for promoting media literacy within the framework of the planning of the state's education policy and the organisation of related areas. For the Ministry of Education and Research, this is not a new task, but a clear definition of the issue and responsibility. In order to better protect the interests of both natural and legal persons, claims made on television or radio shall be subject to an extension of the period of opposition from 20 to 30 days. The draft has supplemented the provisions relating to the prohibition of hate speech by adding a ban on incitement to violence and clarifying their basis. In order to improve access to audiovisual media for disabled people, audiovisual media service providers are obliged to draw up accessibility action plans. The impact and effectiveness of this measure will be assessed by the Ministry of Culture in 2024 and, on the basis of the evaluation results, will develop precise accessibility requirements for the following year, which will be established by a decree of the Minister of Culture. In order to stimulate the creation of audiovisual services in Europe, a quota of at least 30% of the programme catalogue will be introduced for on-demand audiovisual media service providers in Europe. In order to ensure the necessary media diversity and media freedom, the need to ensure, to the extent required by the Directive, the independence and capacity required for the performance of the tasks of the National Media Supervisory Authority, the Consumer Protection and Technical Surveillance Authority and to create the necessary conditions for supervision, for example by extending, inter alia, the storage periods for broadcast recordings, depending on the type of licence required for the provision of television or radio services from 20 days to 30 to 90 days. The draft was drawn up with the participation of representative organisations with a bearing on the subject and defending the interests of the field: the Estonian Chamber of Disabled People, the Estonian Broadcasting Association, the Estonian Society of Information Technology and Telecommunications and the Estonian Association of Media Enterprises, as well as the audiovisual media service providers that were authorised at the time or who submitted a notification of economic activity. In addition, Ekspress Media AS was included, whose main news portals Delfi is managed by Elfi can qualify as an audiovisual media service within the meaning of the Media Services Act with its own Delfi TV, as well as Postimees Grupp AS. The amendments to the Audiovisual Media Services Directive were presented to those involved in September 2019 and written feedback was gathered on the draft legislation on the basis of them in February 2020. Stakeholders proposed, for example, how to better protect minors from inappropriate media content, as well as on issues relating to access to audiovisual media services for people with disabilities and to television and radio advertising. To date, an additional round of involvement of target groups has also been carried out under the leadership of the Cultural Committee of the Riigikogu. Within this framework, the Estonian Chamber of Disabled People and the Estonian Broadcasting Association have submitted their proposals for the draft to give even stronger visibility to the questions of accessibility of media services, and the second to adapt the regulations concerning the mandatory minimum volume and duration of licences in news programmes. Discussions on these proposals are still ongoing. This bill has also been linked to a violation of freedom of expression. However, a distinction must be made between the abuse of freedom of expression and the violation of freedom of expression. Freedom of expression is not an absolute right because it is always accompanied by obligations and responsibilities. It's a question of whether they're restricting anyone's freedom of speech. Any interference with the use of freedom of expression is an obstacle to freedom of expression. Such intervention may be legitimate, i.e. allowed interference or illegal, i.e. violation of freedom of expression. The Media Services Act is about protecting the rights of other people. This is in line with both the European Charter of Fundamental Rights and, for example, the Convention for the Protection of Human Rights and Fundamental Freedoms and the Estonian Constitution. The proportionality of each restriction shall be assessed on a case-by-case basis when the prohibition of incitement to violence and hatred is implemented. Thus, the transposition and implementation of the Audiovisual Media Services Directive in the Estonian legal area does not mean that part of the freedom of expression has automatically been restricted or abolished. That's where I finish the introduction. Thank you and I'm ready to answer your questions. Thank you.

However, a censorship ban does not mean that the state is forbidden to assess the use of freedom of expression and, if necessary, to start proceedings in this regard. I think we teach all children that every act has a consequence and that every act requires responsibility. The same is true of freedom of expression. There is an equal sign between freedom of expression and responsibility and this must be taken into account. I do not remember this wording and maybe I just misinterpreted something, but it is still an agreement between one Member State, Estonia as a Member State of the European Union, and the transposition of the directive is an obligation. We are a member of the European Union. We have agreed to build our media space together. To this end, this Directive has been developed, supplemented by the existing one. And if there is a debate here on whether there is any reason to accept it, then [let it be said] there are also many other amendments and additions to this directive, which I have mentioned. The media landscape is very rapidly evolving, very rapidly changing. We have a large number of channels available, which may not seem to be a threat yet, and we are focusing on the inside of Estonia. But I think that the great value of this directive is also that we can protect our society from what reaches the people of Estonia from the outside. There are certainly service providers whose content creation does not take place in a country of the European Union but who are registered in a country of the European Union. This [Directive] will also provide Estonia with the possibility to intervene under the Directive to bring the service into compliance with the law.

ParlaMint-EE_2021-04-07_U581	Good, that's very nice to hear! Let's get the bull on the horn. The substantive debate started on 9 March. First of all, Minister of Culture Anneli Ott explained the purpose of the draft, and one of the authors of the draft, Siim Rohtla, audiovisual adviser to the Ministry of Culture, presented to the Commission the main amendments to the draft and the aim of the directive. I pointed out that, according to § 19 (1) of the bill, no form of incitement to violence or hatred on grounds of sex, race, colour, ethnic or social origin, genetic characteristics, language, religion or belief, etc., and asked why the list was still such. It says "genetic properties," but it also contains a number of genetic properties, such as sex, race, and skin color. The second question was whether, when providing media services, the programmes could incite violence according to all the characteristics not mentioned in Paragraph 19 (1). Karin Ligi, co-author of the bill, Legal Adviser to the Ministry of Culture, replied that sex, skin colour and race are really genetic traits, but the list in paragraph 1 comes from the Charter of Fundamental Rights of the European Union, which also contains such a list. On that point, I remained firm, and I concluded that all other features of this bill could inspire as much anger as it takes. Moreover, the Charter of Fundamental Rights of the European Union [used] has the word "including" and only then will the list of characteristics be included. This means that the Charter of Fundamental Rights of the European Union prohibits incitement to hatred on the above-mentioned features [with clarification of "including"], but the draft contains only a closed list. The debate on the same subject continued on 15 March when I referred to a directive that had to be transposed and that had to take into account Article 21 of the Charter of Fundamental Rights of the European Union or its basis. I asked whether the list in the Article of the Charter was correctly dealt with if the draft contained only certain features. I said that there is a very large difference between the prohibition of incitement to hatred, discrimination, etc., on the basis of the features listed or, among other things, on the basis of those features. Karin Ligi acknowledged the existence of this difference, but stated that it was based on what was known to be the purpose of this specific provision in the formulation of the directive. I said that it was not stated in the closed list that incitement of hatred, for example by profession or residence or by any other feature, would be prohibited, and I asked whether hatred should be incited against all historians living in Tartu. Peeter Sookruus, Media Services Advisor to the Consumer Protection and Technical Surveillance Board, now intervened and replied that the negotiations and trilogues on the renewal of the directive had this as a subject of heated debates and that there was a reference to Article 21 of the Charter. I said again that it would not be understood why Article 21 of the Charter has not been fully and essentially transposed, so that the draft would also have a formulation "including on the basis of the features listed." Mr Helme asked how the regulation of media services concerns the European Union's competence. According to subsidiarity, this is not a matter for the European Union. Karin Ligi replied that media services had a cross-border character, and Peeter Sookruus added that culture, as such, is a specific regulation within the competence of the Member States, but media and content services are largely linked to the functioning of the internal market. He added that Estonia has been directly involved in the process of drafting the directive, and all the views of the draft have been discussed and approved in depth and under the direction of several governments. He also confirmed that the draft had broad support. Mr Helme said that the draft law clearly deals with regulating the content of the media, which is contrary to the Constitution. Freedom of expression is a fundamental value of the Estonian Constitution. Mr Sookrus argued against the fact that the draft was not a restriction on freedom of expression. All [these standards] have worked for more than 20 years and there is no example in Estonia of restricting freedom of expression or interfering with media freedom and the free movement of information. There is nothing new, Sookruus confirmed, except that the bases of hate speech are slightly expanding. At present, our law only contains a ban on incitement to hatred, but now there is also a ban on incitement to violence and terrorist acts. According to him, this would enable social stability to be better ensured. These standards have been in place for over 20 years, worked smoothly, and no one can bring out any conflict. Martin Helme asked why a new law is needed in this case, if Estonian law has all the standards and Estonia has also ratified the European Convention on cross-border television. Karin Ligi replied that when transposing the directive, it was an obligation to indicate in which national rules the provisions of the directive are in question. The provision, which corresponds exactly to the specific provision of the directive, does not currently exist in Estonian law, Karin Ligi claimed. In Estonia, these principles are derived from several international treaties and even the constitutions, but the precepts cannot be made on their basis. If Estonia wants to prevent real incitement to violence and hatred, there must be a legal basis for this. Mr Helme said that the draft law provides a list of what is discrimination, but it is a loose wording and it is impossible to say what is actually prohibited. I, too, have now said that Peter Sookruus claims that the draft does not concern freedom of expression, and I referred to its author, Siim Rohtla, who had said on 15 February in the "Actual Camera" that the Media Services Act certainly restricts freedom of expression, but it is done for a better and safer society. I asked whether, in the opinion of the advocates of the bill, freedom of expression was restricted or still not restricted. Peter Sootruus reiterated that for more than 20 years these standards have generally worked in this area and there have been no problems. Mr Helme said that the main problem is that some people somewhere decide whether or not there is freedom of expression. The directive says that Member States must transpose it and impose restrictions. This is a law that muzzles the media. He asked how this procedure is conducted and what sanctions are, for example, when Estonian Radio is complaining about Finland or Sweden, and what happens if Estonia does not impose sanctions. Karin Ligi confirmed that the bill certainly does not violate freedom of expression. Censorship is prohibited by the Constitution and no censorship will be introduced. It is not true that other countries can and must complain. Mr Helme expressed the opinion that it was the discretion of an official to open proceedings, which is the penalty procedure. Peter Sookrus replied that all regulation on cross-border services was based on one central principle, the country of origin principle. On 15 March, the same topic was continued and Martin Helme asked what supervisory practices were. Peter Sookrus replied that there had been no case that could be classified as a violation of the ban on incitement to hatred or of the ban on hate speech. All media service providers have developed software to date. These systems work well and there is no more violation. Mr Helme replied that discrimination and incitement to hatred were not so easily measured and not solved by computer software. Helme asked who these people are and what will be their working rules on the basis of which possible discrimination against someone will be measured. Peter Sookrus replied that there were no new issues, only that the ban on incitement to hatred was added to the ban on incitement to violence and terrorist acts. But news and opinion portals Lens and New News as a whole probably will not fall within the scope of the Media Services Act. I guess I won't. The draft concerns, in particular, audiovisual media, claimed by Sookruus. Mr Helme pointed out that the TTJA did not have the competence to deal with the process of monitoring discrimination or incitement to hatred and asked how the TTJA would deal with this monitoring. Peter Sookruus replied that TTJA, formerly also the Ministry of Culture, was the competent authority to deal with it, but there have been no cases so far. Martin Helme asked what would happen if there was a violation of the law. What's this procedure going to look like? Peter Sookrus replied that in the event of an infringement, TTJA would initiate the procedure, examine the situation and assess the situation. Sanctions already exist in the current law and there are no fundamental changes to be made. The Director-General of TTJA Kaur Kajak explained that the media regulator in Estonia is led by Peeter Sookruus, and there are also the head of the Communication Department and two practitioners. If someone gives a hint to the Agency or the Agency itself discovers an infringement, the complaint shall be examined and interviews conducted, which may last for months. Kaur Kajak added that there are 17 and 27 people working at the Latvian and Lithuanian regulatory authorities, and there are only three people in the Estonian institution. Martin Helme now asked what were the sanctions, and Kaur Kayak replied that this was an administrative sentence. The maximum amount of the penalty shall be EUR 32000. Another option is to prohibit the transmission of a broadcast by precept and this depends on the circumstances. Helme asked if TTJA could take away the entire channel pass. Peter Sookrus replied that it would also be possible to conclude that the authorisation would be suspended or revoked. Kaur Kayak added that the TTJA has not yet reached the application of the administrative order. I asked where Estonian society has this problem, which this new legislation wants to solve and which could not be solved under the current law. Helme asked for clarification of those institutions and topics which were not covered by the current law, but are now [on the basis of this draft law] to be included. Karin Ligi replied that there were many changes to the bill. Their aim is to bring Estonia's legal space into line with the changed market conditions. I asked whether, in the meantime, Estonia's media literacy has become worse or whether there has been a problem of incitement to anger on video-sharing portals, which has not been the case so far. Karin Ligi replied that the issue of media literacy is becoming more and more important and that there have been no extreme cases in Estonia according to previous practice does not mean that they cannot come in the future. Mr Helme pointed out that the new law requires a more explicit and verifiable ownership structure. He asked what it wanted. Karin Ligi replied that this specific provision in the directive was voluntary. The requirement for audiovisual media service providers to disclose their ownership structure was not an obligation, but was the will of the previous Minister. Martin Helme asked what ownership structure meant in the first place, and Karin Ligi replied that this means the same data that is now published in the business register, but in the future they would become more easily accessible to those who wish. Now I'm getting to the end. On 22 March, the main topic of media literacy was continued. But already at the meeting on 9 March, I had pointed out that, in relation to the draft, media literacy must be promoted in Estonia, which in turn gives TTJA the role of seeing how the Ministry promotes media literacy. On 9 March, I asked how concerns about media literacy could mean that citizens are taught to prefer certain ideologies instead of source criticism. Karin Ligi stated that it is not true that TTJA can control the role of how the Ministry of Education and Research promotes media literacy. One public authority does not supervise the other. Peeter Sookruus confirmed that the role of double oversight of the Ministry of Education and Research is not and will not be exercised. But TTJA and the Ministry of Education and Research have a busy contact about school programs, how to use the media critically and how to make choices yourself. I referred to the chapter on "Promotion of Media Competence" in the draft, which specifically states that compliance with the requirements of this Act and the legislation established on the basis thereof will be subject to national supervision by the Consumer Protection and Technical Surveillance Authority. According to Karin Ligi, the fact that one administration does not supervise the other administration is a result of other laws. On 22 March, I pointed out the statistics on monitoring media coverage, which proved that Estonian Public Broadcasting is very strongly out of balance with regard to the attitudes of society, and asked the Minister of Education and Research how the Ministry ensures that training in media literacy does not turn into ideological brainwashing in these circumstances.

ParlaMint-EE_2021-04-07_U615	Thank you for that question. Whether or not he has Marxist roots depends in fact on whether or not we consider the regime of the Soviet Union to be based on Marxist ideology. Indeed, this concept of hate speech and discrimination dates from the Soviet Union, already in Stalin's time. Soviet leaders, yes, themselves claimed that they were relying on Marxist ideology, but modern historians are arguing about it, and perhaps social scientists are also arguing about whether the Soviet regime was so Marxist. But the origin of the concept of hate speech is also a very clear article on the weather network; it is an article by Chris Berg, the political director of the Melbourne Institute of Public Relations. Anyone can watch it, it's on the website of this institute. The author describes how the Soviet Union tried to introduce this concept as early as the UN Universal Declaration of Human Rights in 1948, but then failed. It was tried to push in precisely to nullify freedom of expression. During Stalin's time, this failed, but in 1966, when Brezhnev was in power, it succeeded in introducing the UN Covenant on Civil and Political Rights into the International Covenant. The conclusion is that, in any case, the [concept], how to say, birth was not aimed at the release of minorities, as today's human rights activists claim, who say hate speech is good for minorities to feel better, but in fact it was born to control the democrats.

ParlaMint-EE_2021-04-07_U654	Good meeting manager! Ladies and gentlemen, All right, technical personnel! As I have already said in the Commission's report, I am strongly opposed to this draft being approved here at first reading. I'll start with a little more general. You see, freedom of expression is one of the most important elements of democracy. If there is no freedom of expression, there is no democracy, and vice versa. I am afraid that you will not find any authoritarian regimes in the world with freedom of expression, even if you look well. The reason is simply that freedom of expression breaks down the autocratic regimes, at least when individualisation has reached a certain level among members of society, just as disclosure destroyed the Communist regime of the Soviet Union. As you know, and as has already been mentioned in this debate, freedom of expression can be restricted in many different ways. In the case of authoritarian regimes, freedom of expression is directly restricted by pre- or post-censorship in the press. But in semi-authoritarian regimes, it is also enough to create fear of the consequences in order to prevent people and journalists from exercising their right to express their thoughts. The freedom of expression of the media can also be restricted simply by the requirement to be politically correct. Using the concept of incitement to hatred is precisely a fear of the consequences, because it is a vague, broad concept that leaves a framework for interpretation. I would also like to point out that this concept originates in the Soviet Union and should be used as little as possible in our laws, better than at all, at least until it is unambiguously and very clearly defined. Another reason to oppose this draft is that the draft has reached a deadlock in the implementation of this concept, as it contains a closed list of characteristics whose carriers must not be instigated by hatred. Despite the fact that the authors of the draft claim that incitement to hatred against anyone is prohibited. In other laws, however, this now means that people with certain characteristics or groups of people are placed in a privileged position. The authors of the draft act have argued that these features have been taken over from the Charter of Fundamental Rights of the European Union. That's not really true. The Charter of Fundamental Rights mentions these features among others, but the draft contains a closed list. And a closed list, i.e. the priority of groups of people with the same characteristics, such as the most advanced class in the Soviet Union, proletariat, means that attempts are made to change social consciousness and to contrast some people with others. This is totally unacceptable to us Conservatives, to us who see society as an organic whole. And not only for us, it should also be reluctant to all other democratically-minded people and politicians who recognise people's equality. Thirdly, it is unacceptable for us to have the TTJA, which has a wide scope for its application, from the supervision of railway safety to the resolution of consumer disputes, as a censor. There are 120 employees and one of them is truly a media service expert. It is highly doubtful, even if we had clear definitions that this agency would be able to make competent decisions on this matter, decisions that would push our society, that is, Estonia's history, culture and identity. Fourthly, it is unacceptable to us that the TTJA is, according to the draft, separate from the Republic of Estonia on this issue. The agency is therefore independent of the democratic choices of our citizens, of all our choices, ladies and gentlemen, and this deviates from democracy. On the other hand, the Agency is obliged to follow the guidelines of the European Commission. However, we do not support the transfer of competence to the European Union, i.e. the federalisation of the European Union, i.e. the alienation of our citizens from power. We do not support it, it is not democratic. Each of these four reasons is sufficient to reject the draft. But there are five other reasons. It is by approving the draft that we do not yet know the content. These guidelines, which explain the nature of promoting media literacy, must only come from the European Commission. I ask if it is not humiliating if we, who are elected legislators, are acting instead as providers of blank bonds. So, yes, freedom of expression, other civil rights and parliamentary competences will all be lost at the same time when an authoritarian coup is taking place. I don't want to dramatize here, and I don't want to say that in any way. However, this is a step in the wrong direction. ParlaMint-EE_2021-04-07_U659	We are going to the fourth item on today's agenda, which is the draft law on the amendment of the Emergency Act initiated by the Government of the Republic and related amendments to other laws 350. On behalf of the Government of the Republic, the Minister for Economic Affairs and Infrastructure, Taavi Aas, will give a first-reading presentation. Please!
ParlaMint-EE_2021-04-07_U660	[[Remote connection]] Mr President, I would like to thank you very much. Happy Members of the Riigikogu! I will introduce you to the draft Act amending the Emergency Act and related amendments to other laws. The draft is divided into three parts of its content, including provisions on the transfer of crisis management from the Ministry of Internal Affairs to the State Chancellery and on the extension of the competence of the Rescue Board by means of evacuation. As regards the part of the draft relating to the creation of stocks, according to the current work organisation, each ministry prepares risk analyses according to the knowledge of its area of responsibility and seeks funding independently to ensure continuity and build up stocks, which is why the sector's capacities vary greatly in the areas of administration. Compliance with the obligation to build up a reserve depends on the possibility of financing the state budget each year. In a situation where there is no acute need for the stock for a long time, the current solution will result in a marginalisation of commitments. The external analysis on which the draft law was based also confirmed that the responsibilities between the various authorities are fragmented and there is no clear understanding of the purpose, the amount and the amount of resources to be provided. There is no comprehensive solution for both the introduction of stocks and long-term stable financing. Therefore, the current distributed responsibility has led to a situation where there is not enough margin. In the event of an emergency, we have focused on setting up a reserve and ensuring the availability of a reserve, spending significantly more time and financial resources to build up a reserve. Businesses also deliver under tough conditions of competition and optimise their costs. As a result, critical goods may only be available in stock for a few days. Operators shall not prepare for cases which may occur once every 20 years. This, of course, cannot be resented by them in conditions of competition. The outbreak of the Corona virus only aggravated our security of supply shortcomings because cross-border movement is limited, but it is necessary to protect its people and ensure their food. The state must have unused stocks at all times so that we do not run out of personal protective equipment too quickly, food shelves are empty, borders are closed or demand on the market is over-scoring to supply. It is therefore reasonable and necessary to bring the establishment of state resources together in a single institution. To this end, this draft Emergency Act specifies the basis for the establishment, management and deployment of the state's operational reserve and the division of responsibility between the stock manager, ministries and the Government of the Republic. With the draft, we include a chapter on state resources in the Emergency Act, so that there is a clear basis for centralising the establishment and management of stocks. During the discussion of the government's cabinet, it was agreed that the central source of the state's operating resources would be the State-owned company AS Eesti Liquid Fuel Supply Agency, which has 15 years of experience in establishing and maintaining liquid fuel reserves. Following the entry into force of the amendments to the Emergency Act, we will expand the functions of the state company and give it a new name, AS Eesti Tarvaste Keskus. In addition to managing fuel supply, the Estonian Stockpiling Centre will also manage the logistics of other operating resources necessary for the state, such as food, medicines, personal protective equipment, storage, quality control, renewal and deployment, as well as the continuity and daily cooperation of companies that are important for the state reserve. The volume, schedule and method of formation of the reserve are agreed at government level through the annexes to the National Defence Action Plan and in the state budget process, by allocating funds to the Estonian Stock Exchange Centre. The company shall report to the State by publishing at least twice a year a detailed report on the composition, quantity, expiry dates and location of the reserve. The reserve is made up of the AS Estonian Stock Centre, based on the decisions of the Government of the Republic of Estonia, sectoral laws, the input from the risk analyses of ministries and their sub-offices concerning the formation of the reserve and the state's defence action plan. In addition, sectoral working groups will be set up with representatives of both the state and economic operators. It will be reasonable to involve companies as much as possible in the reconstruction of the state's security of supply in the future so that we can take into account their daily experiences, weaknesses and strengths. The aim of the working groups is, inter alia, to find solutions to enhance business continuity and meet the challenges of supply difficulties, to work together to improve service continuity and to carry out exercises and information campaigns. As a general rule, it is reasonable for the inventory centre to conclude delegated stock contracts with economic operators. This means that companies have an agreed amount of stock at any time, it is of high quality and the Estonian Stock Centre has the right to buy it at any time. In summary, the main task of the inventory centre is to form, maintain, renew and ensure their rapid deployment. The composition and composition of the reserve shall be decided in cooperation between the authorities and the stock centre in accordance with the sectoral crisis plans. The Government of the Republic shall decide on the mobilisation of the reserve. As regards the transfer of crisis regulation policy from the Ministry of the Interior to the State Chancellery, under the current procedures, security crises are being prepared under the leadership of the State Chancellery. The competence of the State Chancellery is the management of broad-based national defence planning. At the same time, the Ministry of the Interior is responsible for preparing for, and all related to, crises. The aim of the draft is to bring the management of security crises and civil crises together in one place, to the State Chancellery, so that there is a comprehensive view of the preparation and resolution of crises. The amendment provides an opportunity to harmonise the various activities, reduce duplication and reduce the diversification of resources between institutions. By concentrating different crisis-preparation activities under a single authority, there is also a reduction in the possibility that events that go unnoticed or are not prepared for national defence crises and emergency preparedness. If the task of coordinating crisis management as a cross-governmental area is the task of the State Chancellery, this will also lead to the development of corresponding drafts. At the same time, giving legislative powers does not mean that the State Chancellery will implement the policy in the field. Sectoral policy remains to be shaped and implemented by ministries. On the changes to the extensive evacuation order, I can say that according to the current Emergency Act, extensive evacuation is organised by the Police and Border Guard Board. According to the amendment, the Rescue Board will continue to prepare for and organise evacuation, i.e. the main responsibility will be transferred from the Police and Border Guard Board to the Rescue Board. This means that, in essence, the Rescue Board will remain responsible for the general organisation of evacuation: its management, the information of the residents, the organisation of the evacuation sites, etc. In addition, the Rescue Board must continue to organise evacuation preparation, including evacuation plans, and, in cooperation with local authorities, prepare evacuation sites. The Police and Border Guard Board will continue to support the Rescue Board in carrying out the evacuation, for example in traffic management, limiting the danger zone to public order, etc. This change was made for a number of reasons. Coordination and support of the crisis preparedness of citizens and municipalities is already one of the tasks of the Rescue Board. As the municipalities have an obligation to contribute to the organisation of evacuation, it is reasonable to give the Rescue Board the responsibility of organising evacuation, which would be a necessary contact and helping the municipalities in related matters. It is also reasonable to concentrate the organisation of evacuation and shadowing of the population on the same institution, or Rescue Board. This will encourage better planning of resources and reduce duplication. It is also a question of the resources of the Police and Border Guard Board. It is reasonable to share resources with the Rescue Board in dealing with emergencies and national defence activities. I ask your approval in the procedure for the draft Emergency Act, so that, after the corona crisis, planning our population protection and securing the state's resources will also be better organised, and we will face new crises more systematic and stronger than before. Thank you for your attention and I ask you to support this bill.

ParlaMint-EE_2021-04-07_U731	Thank you, Mr President. This is a completely debatable subject to be reorganized. Although there are some problems here too, such as whether it is right to give such a broad competence to a public limited company that deals with everything from fuel to prescription medicines. Well, the competence to keep and conclude contracts certainly still exists in the ministries of industry, and it is not a very costly job that it should necessarily be concentrated in a single national company. So this, too, is a subject worth discussing. But obviously there's your scale effect, and that's a perfectly reasonable point of discussion. However, for the Isamaa Group, problems arise from the other side of the law concerning the redistribution of competences or, rather, the pooling of major competences with the State Chancellery. This process has been one-way before and will continue. As we have heard here today from a representative of the government, it will also be predominantly collective responsibility in crisis management, collective coordination. The minister avoided the word "management." Collective coordination is, in essence, leadership, and behind the coordinated management of this government, the Prime Minister's ears also appear. In fact, it is a dissipation of responsibility, a dissipation of governance, and it certainly does not make sense. And it is not practical, all the more so as to bring such extensive powers to the State Chancellery, that neither the Ministry of the Interior, the Ministry of Defence nor other ministries can remove this competence, but that duplication will take place. In the case of a country as small as Estonia, it certainly does not make sense financially. Secondly, transfer of competence of the Ministry of Interior to the State Chancellery. As the text of the explanatory memorandum states: The competences, powers and tasks of the Ministry of the Interior in coordinating crisis management are transferred to the Government of the Republic and the State Chancellery. Well, we certainly don't support it. And then, what else will be given to the State Chancellery and what needs to be deduced: "The task of drafting legislation will be laid down for the State Chancellery in coordinating the area of government." No. There are so many things in Estonia's life that are between or above several fields. Is it really all that is not even related to the crisis, as I read out of this explanatory memorandum, that cross-cutting legislation is being developed and coordinated by the State Chancellery? It does not make sense and we cannot support it. As far as questions and answers are concerned, we have already talked about giving the Rescue Board extensive powers, which is a complex issue in itself. The fact that the rescuers are now going to take on the duties of police officers and the duties of medical personnel, etc., is not very practical either. This will not increase the quality of crisis management and resolution. ParlaMint-EE_2021-04-07_U736	Our fifth item is the bill number 351 of the Penal Code Amendment Act (non-cash means of payment) initiated by the Government of the Republic. On behalf of the Government, this will be introduced by Minister of Justice Maris Lauri. Please!
ParlaMint-EE_2021-04-07_U737	Mr President, I would like to thank you very much. Good Riigikogu! This draft is quite short and aims to transpose one amendment relating to the European Union Directive. The directive of the European Parliament and of the Council on combating fraud and counterfeiting of non-cash means of payment is then what we should have fully implemented. The deadline for transposition shall be the end of May of this year. As has been said, this directive relates to fraud and counterfeiting of non-cash means of payment. Such tools may include, for example, bank cards as a whole, but also data on the card on a stand-alone basis, as they enable payments to be made online. The Directive provides for liability both for fraud related to a payment instrument and for the unlawful acquisition and/or handling of a payment instrument for the purpose of fraud. Estonia has largely met the requirements of the Directive, but it is necessary to increase the maximum penalty from one year to two years of imprisonment for the composition of the introduction, which is in § 201 of the Penal Code. This increase may raise the question of what will happen in the future. In general, according to the case law of the Supreme Court, penalties are started from the "average point" and are moved upwards or downwards depending on the severity of the sentence. And now, if you raise this upper limit, you might find that some penalties may rise. But it is always up to the court to take into account that the penalty imposed is proportionate to what has been done. So this possible slope may not be large and may not be forthcoming. That is, in fact, the content of the draft. And here is also the addition of one of the technical notes necessary to achieve legal correctness. That's all for me. Thank you.
