ParlaMint-EE_2021-04-07_U749	Dear Vice-Chairman of the Riigikogu, Honourable Members of the Riigikogu! I give an overview of the draft law 358 amending the Law on the Prevention and Control of communicable diseases and the Tax Administration Act. A specific law complements the register of communicable diseases and therefore clarifies the objectives of the register. A clear reference is also made to the need for the Health Board to provide the law enforcement bodies involved in the performance of its tasks with the necessary information to enable the tasks to be carried out. And the Health Board will have the right to transmit close contact data to the health service provider in certain specific situations, such as the risk of an emergency, such as an emergency, for example, an emergency situation, in particular when we are really already facing a serious public health threat in the area of health care. The Tax Administration Act includes the right to disclose to the Health Board and the Ministry of Social Affairs general information related to the workplace, such as the employer of the person and the workplace. I will later explain in more detail why these changes are, according to the government, necessary. The current problem with the Infectious Diseases Registry is that only infectious patients are collected in the register, but negative test results and close contact data of vaccinees are also needed to resolve the crisis. This data is actually available in the internal information systems of the Health Board itself, but data management is fragmented. The crisis has shown that this need for data does not disappear; rather, it is a lasting need. It is therefore appropriate to ensure adequate transparency and legal clarity in the exercise of the basic tasks laid down by law, not only on the basis of the assistance of the Office for Data Processing. A register of communicable diseases is an appropriate place to regulate the missing one, where all communicable diseases have already been consolidated in the past. According to the amendment, the data in the register will only be supplemented in the event of epidemic distribution, i.e. only in the case of epidemic distribution, the persons who have given the negative test, the close contacts and the vaccinees. This data can be used by the Health Board for both epidemiological studies, such as detection of some outbreaks, prevention of the spread of communicable diseases, and monitoring. Close contact helps to determine the level of risk and to assess the actual risk groups in society, as well as the rate of spread and the means of spread of infection in different groups of infections. Similarly, the mapping of close contacts will help to determine the size of the centres and assess the effectiveness of the measures implemented. It is important to know the results of the negative test in order to assess the spread of further infection, to determine the number of possible close contacts and patterns of spread at national level, as well as to assess the occurrence of the threat, i.e. the occurrence of the disease in a specific site, in short, to carry out risk analyses and to provide for appropriate control measures and aid. Immunisation data are essential to assess the effectiveness of different measures, such as how long this protection lasts, as well as to make reasonable decisions, considered decisions in the event of an epidemic, in order to know the coverage of vaccination and to carry out risk assessments across regions across specific sectors. So if we want to take any measures, take any additional information campaigns, we know where we have concerns and where vaccination is well done. In the same way, it helps to monitor and provide the right sectoral guidance on the control of an infectious disease epidemic. The amendment also provides that the Health Board shall provide the law enforcement bodies involved in the performance of their duties with the necessary data from the same register. The Health Board has the right to transmit close contact details to the healthcare provider, which is another major change. The point and the core of the problem is that the Health Board is currently identifying close contacts, but it is not known to be dealing with the treatment of patients, disease prevention and health guidelines. However, this work can be done by health professionals. Unfortunately, however, the family physician is not aware and does not know who is close to his list if the person himself has not provided him with this information. In order to ensure that persons are aware of the risks of contagious diseases, of reasonable health behaviour, of the need for possible medical examinations and, without doubt, of the need for consultation, it is important that family doctors have the necessary information about their list of members: who are ill, who are in close contact. Then he can help and support them in the best way possible. This provision amends the law so that the Health Board has the right to transmit to the health care provider the data of an infected person in the event of an emergency and in the event of an emergency and emergency. This does not apply at the usual time, it does not apply to any other possible viral disease, but applies when there is a risk of epidemic spread or epidemic spread, or when there is already a very special situation, which has been known to exist only once in Estonian history. The third major issue is the granting of the right to process the data of the employee and his/her workplace, as I referred to, the Health Board and the Ministry of Social Affairs. The question is that health professionals are currently not aware of whether a person belongs to a risk group because of the workplace or whether a person's health concerns and certain health risks may be related to the workplace. At the same time, the Ministry of Social Affairs does not have the possibility to produce statistics by immunized people or vaccinated people and jobs, to assess resource needs and the targeting of communication, etc. If we know that there is a higher risk of infection in certain sectors and, at the same time, a lower level of vaccination, this is very important management information on the basis of which, in future, it will be precisely in this area, in that area or in that authority to provide additional vaccination opportunities. The Agency also does not currently have the possibility to identify collective outbreaks in order to assess the risk, the impact and size of the pandemic and, in specific cases, to provide precise codes of conduct, to carry out epidemiological risk analysis and risk assessment on trends in the spread of communicable diseases. These are the concerns that have emerged during the first and second waves, in the work of establishing measures for inter-agency communication, monitoring them and reporting and targeted actions, in order to maximise support for maintaining human health and increasing the coverage of vaccination. With this amendment, we give the Ministry of Social Affairs and the Health Board the right to process certain personal data by means of a register. As I was referring to, in particular, it is not health data, but general information about the person, the identity of his employer and the identity of his post. This information will provide an opportunity for significantly more precise implementation of possible restrictions, possible measures as well as, in particular, supporting actions to reduce the risk of the virus and increase the number of vaccinees in different sectors. It was a brief overview of the changes planned. I'd be happy to answer your questions. Thank you.

ParlaMint-EE_2021-04-07_U760	Mr President-in-Office of the Council. Minister, thank you very much. Where is the problem in Estonian society that the new regulation seeks to solve and which could not be solved under the current law?
ParlaMint-EE_2021-04-07_U761	Thank you for your question. The problem, as I referred to earlier, is the clear fragmentation of databases. The Health Board collects a lot of different data due to epidemic control, it is information about close contacts or about those who have taken a negative test, but there is no central approach. There is also no legal clarity as to how these data are processed and how long they will be retained. If we create a more comprehensive, clearer legislative base, as well as improvements to the relevant register, all these basic regulations of the register will, of course, also be coordinated with the Data Protection Inspectorate. It is considered that all the conditions for the storage of these data are justified both in the view of security requirements and in terms of time limits, it would be reasonable that no one's data should be kept longer than necessary and not on a temporary, incomprehensible basis, so-called ad-hoc databases. There is a clear central approach that will help, as has been said, to react operationally both during this crisis and during future crises and to implement immediately the epidemiological studies needed for the eradication of the virus, as well as containment measures, more successfully. And the other side, as I have pointed out, concerns all this in favour of supporting close contacts. The question is that, unfortunately, these data are currently not moving within the health sector. I have pointed out before that the Health Board is involved in the size of 200-250 of its employees and in a somewhat additional workforce. But we have had moments where there are tens of thousands of close contacts in Estonia. And these tens of thousands of people need advice, need certain consultations, the better to be given by their family doctor or family nurse, but under the current law, family doctors do not have the right to receive this information from the Health Board. They will only receive this information once the close contact has made itself known. Therefore, people will inevitably receive less and less necessary advice, less care to mitigate health risks than they could get. And, as I was referring to, these specific provisions concerning the transmission of data to family doctors and health authorities in general would apply precisely in times of emergency, emergency and emergency. In particular, we are talking about contacts between infected people, or close contacts in common language. These are some of the gaps that the current Code has not allowed for effective enforcement. These are the places that have emerged during this crisis. One might even say that, especially now, during the second wave, because these volumes and scales are just so much larger. Apparently, you've all seen the news and heard that in the meantime, just the Health Board couldn't do the job anymore, precisely because of the limited human resources. We know that 2,000 professional health professionals are working in teams of family doctors and family nurses from all over Estonia who know and know their patients. It would certainly be more effective to advise people in close contact, to answer health concerns and questions. Health authorities are not really health professionals and do not have such a broad competence in solving people's treatment problems.

There is no bill written by one person from the beginning to the end, at least I don't know that. Maybe one day in the early days of the Republic, that might have been the case. Draft laws shall be drawn up by ministries in cooperation with the relevant agencies and other bodies. In this particular draft, the circle of partners is, of course, wide. On my desk it arrived at the regular weekly meeting of the Ministry of Social Affairs, where we usually review over 5 to 10, between 15 to 20 legal acts, these are draft legislation initiatives, answers to official letters, including questions asked by members of the Riigikogu and written questions. It's a normal process. If you ask me if I was interested in who the person who first came up with the need to supplement the Law on the Prevention and Control of communicable diseases or the Tax Administration Act, I did not ask that question. These processes generally take the form of collective work to address the concerns expressed in normal or crisis situations. What matters to me is whether the proposals for amendments are justified in substance, whether they are considered, whether they are intended for alternatives, which are the current solutions, which will improve, which are danger points. These are the questions, these are the concerns that every minister must take into account if he brings any draft law before Parliament and before the government. These discussions, of course, were held. They were very broad-based, between several ministries and also data protection experts. It was only after that that the draft came to the government and from there before Parliament.

The same data collected on people in the health information system will be taken into account. The outcome of the corona test, close contact or vaccination for the employer, neighbour or schoolmate, for example, has become public and even mandatory information. It is as if human health data were not sensitive personal data that were not yet allowed to be passed on to outsiders, even though they were still there. The current government is secretly and dirtyly trying to violate the constitutional rights of our people, through other laws, without blinking at the same time about the rule of law and belief in it. ParlaMint-EE_2021-04-07_U833	Let's move on to item 7. First reading of the draft bill 352 of ratification of the Protocol amending the Double Taxation Convention on Income and Capital Taxes between the Republic of Estonia and the Federal Republic of Germany, initiated by the Government of the Republic of the Republic of Estonia, signed on 29 November 1996. We've got Secretary of Finance Keit Pentus-Rosimannus as a waitress.
ParlaMint-EE_2021-04-07_U834	[[Remote connection]] Happy Members of the Riigikogu! The Government of the Republic has submitted to the Riigikogu for ratification a protocol amending the long-listed treaty, the double taxation convention between Estonia and Germany signed in 1996. The main incentive for the conclusion of this protocol has been the need to bring the agreement into line with the minimum standard developed by the OECD in order to reduce opportunities for base erosion and profit shifting at international level. In order to prevent tax evasion, a general anti-abuse rule is included in the agreement. This rule allows the tax authorities not to apply the benefits provided for in the tax treaty if the facts suggest that the benefit was one of the main purposes of the transaction. The protocol before you will also change the preamble and title of the agreement. The title shall include a reference to this same objective: to prevent tax evasion and fraud. The preamble also states that, in addition to avoiding double taxation, the objective of the tax treaty is to ensure that tax fraud does not result in any non-taxation of revenue. In addition to these amendments, the Protocol supplements Articles 9, 10 and 13 of the Agreement. In Article 9, the Article of the Related Parties, a new paragraph is added, which provides for a counter-correction obligation in another country for transfer pricing adjustments in one country. The dividend article, which is Article 10, specifies the conditions for applying a more favourable rate of income tax in the source country for the distribution of dividends. And in Article 13, the benefit of the disposal of assets in the Article, a new paragraph is added according to which the State in which the property is situated has the primary right to tax the proceeds of the disposal of such holdings, the value of which results at any time during 365 days prior to the disposal to at least 50%, directly or indirectly, of the real estate. I ask for this very important draft to be supported without any doubt. Thank you.
ParlaMint-EE_2021-04-07_U835	Thank you. There is also a question for the rapporteur. Please, Dmitri Dmitriev!

ParlaMint-EE_2021-04-07_U837	[[Remote connection]] Thank you. That's a good question. The answer is that it doesn't affect it. The agreement with Germany does not affect the taxation of dividends paid to non-resident natural persons and this particular agreement does not affect the taxation of dividends paid to the company. These previous regulations remain valid even after the ratification of this protocol.

ParlaMint-EE_2021-04-07_U845	[[Remote connection]] Good deputy speaker! Honourable Riigikogu, I thank you for this opportunity to hold a debate in Parliament on this important subject, despite the late hour of the evening. Last July, a month after the devastating health crisis reached Europe, the Heads of State and Government of the European Union agreed on an extraordinary package of funding for European Union policies from 2021 to 2027. It was clear that the deep health crisis, which was accompanied by a sharp recession, required rapid and vigorous behaviour, required joint action. The creation of a re-launch plan for the EU of EUR 750 billion is important, not only in the context of overcoming the coronavir situation. It is a significant shift towards a truly united and united Europe, towards the European Union. It is no secret or surprise that for Estonia, the survival and functioning of the European Union, recovery and new growth are existential interests. Such a fragmented European Union, where everyone would only fight for themselves and keep their fingernails to themselves, would be disastrous for us, for Estonia, whether it is the view of the economy, security or the everyday life and everyday life of people. The re-launch plan, which will help those countries that have been most hurt for independent reasons, in the post-crisis situation or, in fact, in the crisis that is still ongoing, is the most hit, and the entire facility, which is financed jointly, will certainly make the European Union stronger and also make the future more secure. This decision made last year in the heart of summer is, you can safely say, also in the direct interests of Estonia. Out of the re-launch plan, EUR 390 billion will be channelled through the EU budget as grants to Member States in order for the economies of the Member States and of the European Union as a whole to grow and emerge from the crisis as a strong one. In essence, it was decided, through joint lending in the short term, to leverage the European Union's budget by more than a third so that not only the Member States, but also the European Union itself, would react countercyclically to the recession that has arisen. Almost ten months ago, on 15 June 2020, the then Head of Government, Prime Minister, also gave Parliament a comprehensive overview of the re-launch plan and its funding. So this debate has been held in Parliament several years ago. It must now be said that the first quarter of 2021 is over, the crisis is still with us, but what the citizens of the European Union still do not see is the money promised for relaunching Europe. That is why this debate is also taking place today, and this draft has been submitted to you. The European Commission cannot borrow the relaunch money on behalf of the European Union on the market until all Member States have actually completed their national actions to approve the own resources decision. The first payments under this joint loan would be made to countries in the summer, provided that all Member States have approved this decision by then. As of today, 13 Member States have ratified the decision and it is known that the majority of Member States are planning to reach this decision by the end of May. Understandably, the mandate of the Riigikogu is equally necessary. Without the approval of the Riigikogu, the plan to relaunch the European Union cannot be implemented or implemented. The European Union's re-launch plan is, in itself, a good proof or a sign of the European Union's solidarity, that the countries most hit by the crisis, who have suffered the most and are still suffering the most from the crisis, will receive more support. At the same time, the burden of repaying the loan still depends mainly on the wealth of the Member State. The greater the share of the country in the total national income of the European Union, the greater the burden on that country to repay the loan. Estonia will receive support in the order of up to 0.8% of GDP in six years. This is, according to current knowledge and calculations, a total indicative amount of EUR 1.5 billion. The support given to Estonia is 0.39% of the total amount of grants under the European Union Re-launch Package, while our average refund burden will be approximately 0.26%. Why do I highlight these numbers or bring them out? If anyone, let's say, cynically tries to approach or make calculations in this key, then [it should be taken into account that] the re-launch plan is not only that the European Union benefits collectively, but also by looking at Estonia separately, it is literally profitable. The support available to us is about 48% higher than we will pay back in a long time. It is also very important to acknowledge that this borrowing is common only in the sense that the European Commission will have the power to issue bonds on behalf of the Union for the implementation of common policies. In other words, no Member State guarantees the repayment obligation of another country; each country is responsible for its own part of the repayment. If a Member State were to decide to take a loan of up to EUR 360 billion from the loan instrument of the re-launch scheme of the European Union in addition to the loan facility, this option would not, of course, affect the budget of the European Union or affect other Member States at all. The repayment of a loan to increase the EU budget's expenditure policies by 390 billion will take place on a consistent schedule over more than 30 years, through a contribution to the EU budget. A long schedule of loans benefiting from a favourable interest environment allows for a very long repayment period and the cost of servicing the loan is likely to be less than 5% on average of the budget expenditure of the European Union. Good Riigikogu! The own resources decision that you have before you also contains another important change that I would like to introduce. For the first time since Estonia's accession, a new source of income, a new own resource, is an own resource based on the quantity of plastic packaging that has not been recycled, is also introduced to finance the budget of the European Union. The debate on new sources of income itself has certainly been on everyone's eye, it has been going on for a long time, it has been going on for years. The voice of the European Parliament and its role in standing up for new own resources have also become increasingly strong. Why is it necessary to do such a thing in the first place, or why is it necessary? To sum up, this is, among other things, a desire to stimulate or motivate the achievement of the European Union's common policy objectives through the collection of European Union revenue. In any case, nature, environment and climate protection are also important objectives of the European Union. At the same time, plastic packaging, which is not recycled but which ends up either in nature or in a dump, is actually becoming one of the major global environmental problems. This is an issue that certainly needs intervention at both Member State and European Union level. Data on packaging recycling definitely need critical clean-up. According to current knowledge, in 2018, i.e. the last year we currently have data on, more than 55000 tonnes of plastic packaging waste and 35000 tonnes of these waste were not recycled, i.e. almost 60% is the part that Estonia could not recycle. It has to be acknowledged that, as of now, we are weaker than average in our recycling capacity in the European Union. By comparison: the European Union average is about 42%, it was about 42% in 2018, but in Estonia this percentage was about 38. This is certainly a type of problem that, above all, requires a rather decisive and broad change in behaviour. The new own resource of the European Union, the amount of which, as well as the amount paid by the State to the budget of the European Union, still depends specifically on the amount of plastic packaging recycled in a Member State, is an additional incentive which, in its own way, forces Member States, for practical, financial reasons, to find faster solutions to one major environmental problem. The new package-based own resource in itself increases Estonia's contribution to the budget of the European Union. Based on 2018 data, it is estimated that this increase will be around EUR 11 million per year. But, of course, it is true that the more ambitious we are, the more capable we are of recycling packaging waste, the less our contribution will increase. So this should, hopefully, be a dwindling and environmentally significant resource and source of revenue that will make the Member States act faster and more efficiently. Honourable members of the Riigikogu, I ask you to support the draft before you. This decision also depends on how quickly we can adopt the recovery plan and the recovery facility. Thank you.
